[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I’m here because of elevators, mostly. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, because I understand they’re not actually dangerous most of the time, and people use them all day without thinking twice. But when I have to get in one, I just get really scared, like I’m going to be trapped in there and I won’t be able to get out. I can’t seem to talk myself into feeling normal about it, even though I know in my head it doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar feelings of fear or anxiety in situations other than being in elevators?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the same way. Like, I get nervous about normal stuff sometimes, like anyone does, but that trapped feeling and the panic-y feeling is really tied to elevators for me. I’ve tried to think if there’s something else that sets it off the same way, but I can’t really come up with anything. It’s mostly when I’m actually facing getting in one, or even thinking I might have to use one.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: It’s been about… I’d say around 11 months now. It started after we lost the house and had to move into an upper-floor apartment. Before that, I mean, I never loved elevators or anything, but it wasn’t like this. Once we moved there and using the elevator became part of getting in and out, that’s when it really started becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, which may have included symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does come on really fast when it happens. It’s not like I sit there getting nervous for an hour and then it builds up slowly. It’s more like the second I’m actually in the elevator and those doors start sliding shut, my body just reacts. My chest gets tight right away, and my hands start sweating, and I get this really strong thought of, “I need to get out. I need out now,” even if I know it’s only going up a few floors and it’ll probably be over in less than a minute.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because part of me is standing there trying to act normal, especially if other people are in there, but inside I’m just focused on the doors and wanting them to open again. It peaks pretty quickly, I guess—like within moments of the doors closing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or scared in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or crowded rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can be uncomfortable if a place is really packed or if I’m in a car too long, but it’s not the same thing. The elevator is the thing that gets me. I know in my head elevators aren’t really dangerous, and people use them all day without anything happening, but when I have to get in one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe that.\n\nIt’s more that I feel like I’m going to be trapped in there, like once the doors close I can’t just leave if I want to. That’s the part that scares me. And I can tell myself, “You’re fine, it’s just an elevator,” but I can’t make myself feel normal about it. So cars or crowded rooms don’t really do that to me the way elevators do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically about being in an elevator, or is it also about the possibility of something going wrong or being trapped in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the elevator, but… I guess the trapped part is the center of it, if that makes sense. Like, I know elevators aren’t really dangerous. I know that logically. People ride them all day, every day, and most of the time nothing happens.\n\nBut when I’m the one who has to step in, and the doors close, it’s like my mind goes straight to, “What if I can’t get out?” I start feeling like I’m stuck in there, even if it’s only supposed to be a short ride. I can’t make myself feel normal about it once I’m inside. So it’s not really that I think the elevator is going to crash or anything specific like that—it’s more the feeling of being closed in and not being able to leave when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this trapped feeling in any situation other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can feel stuck in a normal life sense, like everyone does, but that trapped feeling where my whole body reacts… that’s really with elevators.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I know elevators aren’t really dangerous. I can say that out loud and I believe it in my head. But when I actually have to get in one, especially once the doors close, I get scared like I’m going to be trapped in there and I won’t be able to get out. And then I can’t make myself feel normal about it, even if I’m telling myself it’s fine. It’s like the elevator makes that fear feel real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided other situations because you felt they might trigger a similar feeling of being trapped or losing control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly if it involves an elevator or one of those lifts. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding a bunch of different things, like it’s not every closed-in situation, but I do plan around elevators a lot more than I probably want to admit.\n\nAt my apartment, most days I just take the stairs. Even if I’m tired or carrying stuff, I’ll still talk myself into the stairs because it feels easier than dealing with that feeling when the elevator doors close. And on job sites, it comes up too. If there’s stairs, I’m using them. If the only way up is an elevator or a lift, then I kind of freeze up about it. I’ve asked for different work before, or tried to switch with somebody, just so I don’t have to go up that way.\n\nIf I absolutely have to ride it, I do better if somebody I trust is right there with me. Like, if a coworker I know is standing next to me, I can sometimes make myself do it, but I’m still not comfortable. It’s more like I’m enduring it, not like I’m fine. So yeah, I guess I do avoid things when I think they’re going to bring on that same trapped feeling, but it’s mainly when it’s elevators or lifts involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in other small, enclosed spaces when you couldn't easily leave, such as a small room without a clear exit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, because I know you mean small rooms or places where there isn’t an easy way out, but I can’t say I’ve noticed the same reaction there.\n\nIf I’m in a little room, or somewhere cramped, I might not love it, but it doesn’t hit me the way an elevator does. With the elevator, it’s something about the doors closing and knowing I can’t just step out whenever I want. A small room usually still feels like… there’s a door, I can leave, or I can open it. So I don’t think I’ve had that same trapped panic in other spaces like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar intense fear or panic in a situation where you felt confined or restricted, but not in an elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think, because I don’t want to say no too fast if I’m forgetting something, but… no, I don’t think I’ve had that same intense panic outside of elevators.\n\nI mean, there are times I don’t like feeling boxed in, like if a space is tight or there are people around and it’s hard to move, but it doesn’t become that same thing in my body. It doesn’t hit that level where I feel like, oh no, I can’t get out, I’m stuck. With elevators it’s very specific — the doors shut, it starts moving, and I know I can’t just leave right that second. That’s when my fear really spikes.\n\nIf I’m somewhere else that’s confined, usually I can remind myself there’s a door, or I can step away, or it just doesn’t feel the same. So, as far as I can tell, no… not the same kind of intense fear or panic, not unless an elevator or lift is involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in any situation where you couldn't escape or leave immediately, aside from being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve been trying to think about that because it sounds like maybe I should have some other example, but I honestly don’t think I do.\n\nLike, there are normal times where you can’t leave right away — standing in a line, being in traffic, being in a crowded area at work or something — and I might get annoyed or uncomfortable, but it doesn’t turn into that trapped feeling. It’s not the same thing in my chest or my head. I don’t get that sudden “I have to get out right now” feeling unless it’s the elevator or a lift kind of situation.\n\nIf there’s a door I can walk through, or I know I could step away if I really needed to, I’m usually okay. So aside from elevators, I can’t really point to another situation where I’ve felt that same trapped anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you were confined or restricted, but not in an elevator, such as on a plane or a bus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve been on buses before, and I don’t love when they’re crowded or when you’re kind of stuck waiting for stops, but it doesn’t feel like that trapped feeling I get with an elevator.\n\nA plane… I’m trying to think. It’s not like I’m on planes all the time, but I don’t remember having that same kind of reaction there either. I might be a little uncomfortable just because you’re sitting there and you can’t exactly get off whenever you want, but it’s not the same sudden fear for me.\n\nWith the elevator, it’s more specific — the doors closing, the small space, knowing it’s moving and I can’t just step out. On a bus or a plane, even if I’m technically stuck there for a while, my mind doesn’t seem to react the same way. So no, I haven’t really noticed that outside of elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a situation where you were confined, but it wasn't an elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I know I’ve kind of said that already, but I’m trying to answer it honestly and not just give you the quick answer.\n\nIf it’s just a confined place, like a small room or being somewhere crowded, I might not love it, but I don’t get scared in that same way. I can usually tell myself I can leave, or there’s a door, or it’s just temporary. It doesn’t grab me the same way.\n\nWith an elevator, it’s different because once the doors close, I feel like I can’t do anything about it right then. That’s the part that seems to set me off. But other confined situations… I can’t really think of one where I’ve felt truly scared or panicky like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a tunnel or a bridge, where you couldn't easily turn back or exit?\n\nPatient: Tunnels or bridges… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve driven through tunnels and over bridges, and sometimes you’re kind of committed once you’re on them, especially if there’s traffic, but I can’t remember feeling scared from that.\n\nMaybe a little tense if traffic is bad or if people are driving stupid, but that feels more like normal driving stress, not that trapped feeling. I don’t sit there thinking, “I can’t get out, I need to escape,” or anything like that.\n\nWith bridges especially, I’m usually just paying attention to the road. And tunnels, I guess they’re enclosed, but for some reason they don’t hit me the same way. I don’t really notice myself worrying about not being able to turn back or exit. So… no, I wouldn’t say tunnels or bridges are a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a small, enclosed space with no clear exit, such as a crowded subway car?\n\nPatient: A crowded subway car… I’m not totally sure, because I don’t ride subways much, honestly. If I’m picturing it, I can see how that would be uncomfortable — like if people are pressed in and you can’t move around much — but I don’t know that I’ve had that same scared feeling in that kind of situation.\n\nSmall enclosed spaces in general don’t always bother me just because they’re small. Like, if there’s a door or if I know I can step out, I’m usually okay. Crowds can be annoying, and I don’t like feeling shoved in or like people are too close, but that’s more irritation or wanting some space, not fear.\n\nThe part that really seems different for me is when I feel like I’m sealed in and can’t just get out when I want to. But with something like a subway car, I don’t have a clear memory of panicking or being scared in one. So I guess my answer is… no, not that I’ve noticed, or at least not enough that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you couldn't escape, but it wasn't related to an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I know it might sound like I’m being repetitive, but I’m trying to sort through it in my head because “couldn’t escape” can mean a few different things, you know?\n\nLike, I’ve been in situations where I couldn’t just walk away immediately — traffic, waiting in line, being in a crowded place, being stuck somewhere for work — and I might get annoyed or impatient. But I don’t remember feeling actually trapped or scared in the way I think you mean.\n\nFor me, if it’s not the elevator kind of situation, I can usually reason it out. Like, there’s an exit somewhere, or eventually the line moves, or the traffic clears, or I can step outside if I really need to. It doesn’t turn into that same feeling of, “I have to get out right now.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening outside of elevators.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I can’t make myself feel normal about it once I’m inside. So it’s not really that I think the elevator is going to crash or anything specific like that—it’s more the feeling of being closed in and not being able to leave when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this trapped feeling in any situation other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can feel stuck in a normal life sense, like everyone does, but that trapped feeling where my whole body reacts… that’s really with elevators.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. But when I actually have to get in one, especially once the doors close, I get scared like I’m going to be trapped in there and I won’t be able to get out. And then I can’t make myself feel normal about it, even if I’m telling myself it’s fine. It’s like the elevator makes that fear feel real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided other situations because you felt they might trigger a similar feeling of being trapped or losing control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly if it involves an elevator or one of those lifts. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding a bunch of different things, like it’s not every closed-in situation, but I do plan around elevators a lot more than I probably want to admit.\n\nAt my apartment, most days I just take the stairs. Even if I’m tired or carrying stuff, I’ll still talk myself into the stairs because it feels easier than dealing with that feeling when the elevator doors close. And on job sites, it comes up too. If there’s stairs, I’m using them. If the only way up is an elevator or a lift, then I kind of freeze up about it. I’ve asked for different work before, or tried to switch with somebody, just so I don’t have to go up that way.\n\nIf I absolutely have to ride it, I do better if somebody I trust is right there with me. Like, if a coworker I know is standing next to me, I can sometimes make myself do it, but I’m still not comfortable. It’s more like I’m enduring it, not like I’m fine. So yeah, I guess I do avoid things when I think they’re going to bring on that same trapped feeling, but it’s mainly when it’s elevators or lifts involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in other small, enclosed spaces when you couldn't easily leave, such as a small room without a clear exit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, because I know you mean small rooms or places where there isn’t an easy way out, but I can’t say I’ve noticed the same reaction there.\n\nIf I’m in a little room, or somewhere cramped, I might not love it, but it doesn’t hit me the way an elevator does. With the elevator, it’s something about the doors closing and knowing I can’t just step out whenever I want. A small room usually still feels like… there’s a door, I can leave, or I can open it. It’s mostly when I’m actually facing getting in one, or even thinking I might have to use one.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: It’s been about… I’d say around 11 months now. It started after we lost the house and had to move into an upper-floor apartment. Before that, I mean, I never loved elevators or anything, but it wasn’t like this. Once we moved there and using the elevator became part of getting in and out, that’s when it really started becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, which may have included symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does come on really fast when it happens. It’s not like I sit there getting nervous for an hour and then it builds up slowly. It’s more like the second I’m actually in the elevator and those doors start sliding shut, my body just reacts. My chest gets tight right away, and my hands start sweating, and I get this really strong thought of, “I need to get out. I need out now,” even if I know it’s only going up a few floors and it’ll probably be over in less than a minute.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because part of me is standing there trying to act normal, especially if other people are in there, but inside I’m just focused on the doors and wanting them to open again. It peaks pretty quickly, I guess—like within moments of the doors closing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or scared in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or crowded rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can be uncomfortable if a place is really packed or if I’m in a car too long, but it’s not the same thing. The elevator is the thing that gets me. I know in my head elevators aren’t really dangerous, and people use them all day without anything happening, but when I have to get in one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe that.\n\nIt’s more that I feel like I’m going to be trapped in there, like once the doors close I can’t just leave if I want to. That’s the part that scares me. And I can tell myself, “You’re fine, it’s just an elevator,” but I can’t make myself feel normal about it. So cars or crowded rooms don’t really do that to me the way elevators do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically about being in an elevator, or is it also about the possibility of something going wrong or being trapped in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the elevator, but… I guess the trapped part is the center of it, if that makes sense. Like, I know elevators aren’t really dangerous. I know that logically. People ride them all day, every day, and most of the time nothing happens.\n\nBut when I’m the one who has to step in, and the doors close, it’s like my mind goes straight to, “What if I can’t get out?” I start feeling like I’m stuck in there, even if it’s only supposed to be a short ride. I can’t make myself feel normal about it once I’m inside. So it’s not really that I think the elevator is going to crash or anything specific like that—it’s more the feeling of being closed in and not being able to leave when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this trapped feeling in any situation other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can feel stuck in a normal life sense, like everyone does, but that trapped feeling where my whole body reacts… that’s really with elevators.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I know elevators aren’t really dangerous. I can say that out loud and I believe it in my head. But when I actually have to get in one, especially once the doors close, I get scared like I’m going to be trapped in there and I won’t be able to get out. And then I can’t make myself feel normal about it, even if I’m telling myself it’s fine. It’s like the elevator makes that fear feel real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided other situations because you felt they might trigger a similar feeling of being trapped or losing control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly if it involves an elevator or one of those lifts. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding a bunch of different things, like it’s not every closed-in situation, but I do plan around elevators a lot more than I probably want to admit.\n\nAt my apartment, most days I just take the stairs. Even if I’m tired or carrying stuff, I’ll still talk myself into the stairs because it feels easier than dealing with that feeling when the elevator doors close. And on job sites, it comes up too. If there’s stairs, I’m using them. If the only way up is an elevator or a lift, then I kind of freeze up about it. I’ve asked for different work before, or tried to switch with somebody, just so I don’t have to go up that way.\n\nIf I absolutely have to ride it, I do better if somebody I trust is right there with me. Like, if a coworker I know is standing next to me, I can sometimes make myself do it, but I’m still not comfortable. It’s more like I’m enduring it, not like I’m fine. So yeah, I guess I do avoid things when I think they’re going to bring on that same trapped feeling, but it’s mainly when it’s elevators or lifts involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in other small, enclosed spaces when you couldn't easily leave, such as a small room without a clear exit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, because I know you mean small rooms or places where there isn’t an easy way out, but I can’t say I’ve noticed the same reaction there.\n\nIf I’m in a little room, or somewhere cramped, I might not love it, but it doesn’t hit me the way an elevator does. With the elevator, it’s something about the doors closing and knowing I can’t just step out whenever I want. A small room usually still feels like… there’s a door, I can leave, or I can open it. So I don’t think I’ve had that same trapped panic in other spaces like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar intense fear or panic in a situation where you felt confined or restricted, but not in an elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think, because I don’t want to say no too fast if I’m forgetting something, but… no, I don’t think I’ve had that same intense panic outside of elevators.\n\nI mean, there are times I don’t like feeling boxed in, like if a space is tight or there are people around and it’s hard to move, but it doesn’t become that same thing in my body. It doesn’t hit that level where I feel like, oh no, I can’t get out, I’m stuck. With elevators it’s very specific — the doors shut, it starts moving, and I know I can’t just leave right that second. That’s when my fear really spikes.\n\nIf I’m somewhere else that’s confined, usually I can remind myself there’s a door, or I can step away, or it just doesn’t feel the same. So, as far as I can tell, no… not the same kind of intense fear or panic, not unless an elevator or lift is involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in any situation where you couldn't escape or leave immediately, aside from being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve been trying to think about that because it sounds like maybe I should have some other example, but I honestly don’t think I do.\n\nLike, there are normal times where you can’t leave right away — standing in a line, being in traffic, being in a crowded area at work or something — and I might get annoyed or uncomfortable, but it doesn’t turn into that trapped feeling. It’s not the same thing in my chest or my head. I don’t get that sudden “I have to get out right now” feeling unless it’s the elevator or a lift kind of situation.\n\nIf there’s a door I can walk through, or I know I could step away if I really needed to, I’m usually okay. So aside from elevators, I can’t really point to another situation where I’ve felt that same trapped anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you were confined or restricted, but not in an elevator, such as on a plane or a bus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve been on buses before, and I don’t love when they’re crowded or when you’re kind of stuck waiting for stops, but it doesn’t feel like that trapped feeling I get with an elevator.\n\nA plane… I’m trying to think. It’s not like I’m on planes all the time, but I don’t remember having that same kind of reaction there either. I might be a little uncomfortable just because you’re sitting there and you can’t exactly get off whenever you want, but it’s not the same sudden fear for me.\n\nWith the elevator, it’s more specific — the doors closing, the small space, knowing it’s moving and I can’t just step out. On a bus or a plane, even if I’m technically stuck there for a while, my mind doesn’t seem to react the same way. So no, I haven’t really noticed that outside of elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a situation where you were confined, but it wasn't an elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I know I’ve kind of said that already, but I’m trying to answer it honestly and not just give you the quick answer.\n\nIf it’s just a confined place, like a small room or being somewhere crowded, I might not love it, but I don’t get scared in that same way. I can usually tell myself I can leave, or there’s a door, or it’s just temporary. It doesn’t grab me the same way.\n\nWith an elevator, it’s different because once the doors close, I feel like I can’t do anything about it right then. That’s the part that seems to set me off. But other confined situations… I can’t really think of one where I’ve felt truly scared or panicky like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a tunnel or a bridge, where you couldn't easily turn back or exit?\n\nPatient: Tunnels or bridges… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve driven through tunnels and over bridges, and sometimes you’re kind of committed once you’re on them, especially if there’s traffic, but I can’t remember feeling scared from that.\n\nMaybe a little tense if traffic is bad or if people are driving stupid, but that feels more like normal driving stress, not that trapped feeling. I don’t sit there thinking, “I can’t get out, I need to escape,” or anything like that.\n\nWith bridges especially, I’m usually just paying attention to the road. And tunnels, I guess they’re enclosed, but for some reason they don’t hit me the same way. I don’t really notice myself worrying about not being able to turn back or exit. So… no, I wouldn’t say tunnels or bridges are a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a small, enclosed space with no clear exit, such as a crowded subway car?\n\nPatient: A crowded subway car… I’m not totally sure, because I don’t ride subways much, honestly. If I’m picturing it, I can see how that would be uncomfortable — like if people are pressed in and you can’t move around much — but I don’t know that I’ve had that same scared feeling in that kind of situation.\n\nSmall enclosed spaces in general don’t always bother me just because they’re small. Like, if there’s a door or if I know I can step out, I’m usually okay. Crowds can be annoying, and I don’t like feeling shoved in or like people are too close, but that’s more irritation or wanting some space, not fear.\n\nThe part that really seems different for me is when I feel like I’m sealed in and can’t just get out when I want to. But with something like a subway car, I don’t have a clear memory of panicking or being scared in one. So I guess my answer is… no, not that I’ve noticed, or at least not enough that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you couldn't escape, but it wasn't related to an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I know it might sound like I’m being repetitive, but I’m trying to sort through it in my head because “couldn’t escape” can mean a few different things, you know?\n\nLike, I’ve been in situations where I couldn’t just walk away immediately — traffic, waiting in line, being in a crowded place, being stuck somewhere for work — and I might get annoyed or impatient. But I don’t remember feeling actually trapped or scared in the way I think you mean.\n\nFor me, if it’s not the elevator kind of situation, I can usually reason it out. Like, there’s an exit somewhere, or eventually the line moves, or the traffic clears, or I can step outside if I really need to. It doesn’t turn into that same feeling of, “I have to get out right now.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening outside of elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in a situation where you couldn't escape, but it was not related to an elevator or any mode of transportation?\n\nPatient: Outside of elevators and transportation… I’m trying to think. I don’t get that anxious feeling just from being somewhere enclosed if I know there’s some way out.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really point to a time like that that wasn’t an elevator or a vehicle or something. Um… not related to a place at all? I’m not sure I’ve had that. I mean, I’ve felt stuck in life situations before, like anybody does — bills, work stuff, dealing with people when you can’t just magically make them stop talking — but that’s more stress or frustration, not like that trapped anxious feeling.

When you say “couldn’t escape,” my mind goes straight to actually being physically stuck somewhere, so if you mean something more like a conversation or an obligation or a situation I can’t get out of emotionally, I don’t think it hits me the same way. I might want to be done with it, or I might get irritated, but I don’t feel scared like I can’t breathe or like I need to get out right that second.

So I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve noticed. If it’s not an actual physical stuck kind of thing, I don’t really think of it as feeling trapped.

It started after we lost the house and had to move into an upper-floor apartment. Before that, I mean, I never loved elevators or anything, but it wasn’t like this. Once we moved there and using the elevator became part of getting in and out, that’s when it really started becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, which may have included symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does come on really fast when it happens. It’s not like I sit there getting nervous for an hour and then it builds up slowly. It’s more like the second I’m actually in the elevator and those doors start sliding shut, my body just reacts. My chest gets tight right away, and my hands start sweating, and I get this really strong thought of, “I need to get out. I need out now,” even if I know it’s only going up a few floors and it’ll probably be over in less than a minute.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because part of me is standing there trying to act normal, especially if other people are in there, but inside I’m just focused on the doors and wanting them to open again. It peaks pretty quickly, I guess—like within moments of the doors closing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or scared in other small or enclosed spaces, like cars or crowded rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can be uncomfortable if a place is really packed or if I’m in a car too long, but it’s not the same thing. The elevator is the thing that gets me. I know in my head elevators aren’t really dangerous, and people use them all day without anything happening, but when I have to get in one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe that.\n\nIt’s more that I feel like I’m going to be trapped in there, like once the doors close I can’t just leave if I want to. That’s the part that scares me. And I can tell myself, “You’re fine, it’s just an elevator,” but I can’t make myself feel normal about it. So cars or crowded rooms don’t really do that to me the way elevators do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically about being in an elevator, or is it also about the possibility of something going wrong or being trapped in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the elevator, but… I guess the trapped part is the center of it, if that makes sense. Like, I know elevators aren’t really dangerous. I know that logically. People ride them all day, every day, and most of the time nothing happens.\n\nBut when I’m the one who has to step in, and the doors close, it’s like my mind goes straight to, “What if I can’t get out?” I start feeling like I’m stuck in there, even if it’s only supposed to be a short ride. I can’t make myself feel normal about it once I’m inside. So it’s not really that I think the elevator is going to crash or anything specific like that—it’s more the feeling of being closed in and not being able to leave when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this trapped feeling in any situation other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can feel stuck in a normal life sense, like everyone does, but that trapped feeling where my whole body reacts… that’s really with elevators.\n\nThat’s kind of why I’m here, honestly. I know elevators aren’t really dangerous. I can say that out loud and I believe it in my head. But when I actually have to get in one, especially once the doors close, I get scared like I’m going to be trapped in there and I won’t be able to get out. And then I can’t make myself feel normal about it, even if I’m telling myself it’s fine. It’s like the elevator makes that fear feel real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided other situations because you felt they might trigger a similar feeling of being trapped or losing control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly if it involves an elevator or one of those lifts. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding a bunch of different things, like it’s not every closed-in situation, but I do plan around elevators a lot more than I probably want to admit.\n\nAt my apartment, most days I just take the stairs. Even if I’m tired or carrying stuff, I’ll still talk myself into the stairs because it feels easier than dealing with that feeling when the elevator doors close. And on job sites, it comes up too. If there’s stairs, I’m using them. If the only way up is an elevator or a lift, then I kind of freeze up about it. I’ve asked for different work before, or tried to switch with somebody, just so I don’t have to go up that way.\n\nIf I absolutely have to ride it, I do better if somebody I trust is right there with me. Like, if a coworker I know is standing next to me, I can sometimes make myself do it, but I’m still not comfortable. It’s more like I’m enduring it, not like I’m fine. So yeah, I guess I do avoid things when I think they’re going to bring on that same trapped feeling, but it’s mainly when it’s elevators or lifts involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in other small, enclosed spaces when you couldn't easily leave, such as a small room without a clear exit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, because I know you mean small rooms or places where there isn’t an easy way out, but I can’t say I’ve noticed the same reaction there.\n\nIf I’m in a little room, or somewhere cramped, I might not love it, but it doesn’t hit me the way an elevator does. With the elevator, it’s something about the doors closing and knowing I can’t just step out whenever I want. A small room usually still feels like… there’s a door, I can leave, or I can open it. So I don’t think I’ve had that same trapped panic in other spaces like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar intense fear or panic in a situation where you felt confined or restricted, but not in an elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think, because I don’t want to say no too fast if I’m forgetting something, but… no, I don’t think I’ve had that same intense panic outside of elevators.\n\nI mean, there are times I don’t like feeling boxed in, like if a space is tight or there are people around and it’s hard to move, but it doesn’t become that same thing in my body. It doesn’t hit that level where I feel like, oh no, I can’t get out, I’m stuck. With elevators it’s very specific — the doors shut, it starts moving, and I know I can’t just leave right that second. That’s when my fear really spikes.\n\nIf I’m somewhere else that’s confined, usually I can remind myself there’s a door, or I can step away, or it just doesn’t feel the same. So, as far as I can tell, no… not the same kind of intense fear or panic, not unless an elevator or lift is involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in any situation where you couldn't escape or leave immediately, aside from being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve been trying to think about that because it sounds like maybe I should have some other example, but I honestly don’t think I do.\n\nLike, there are normal times where you can’t leave right away — standing in a line, being in traffic, being in a crowded area at work or something — and I might get annoyed or uncomfortable, but it doesn’t turn into that trapped feeling. It’s not the same thing in my chest or my head. I don’t get that sudden “I have to get out right now” feeling unless it’s the elevator or a lift kind of situation.\n\nIf there’s a door I can walk through, or I know I could step away if I really needed to, I’m usually okay. So aside from elevators, I can’t really point to another situation where I’ve felt that same trapped anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you were confined or restricted, but not in an elevator, such as on a plane or a bus?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve been on buses before, and I don’t love when they’re crowded or when you’re kind of stuck waiting for stops, but it doesn’t feel like that trapped feeling I get with an elevator.\n\nA plane… I’m trying to think. It’s not like I’m on planes all the time, but I don’t remember having that same kind of reaction there either. I might be a little uncomfortable just because you’re sitting there and you can’t exactly get off whenever you want, but it’s not the same sudden fear for me.\n\nWith the elevator, it’s more specific — the doors closing, the small space, knowing it’s moving and I can’t just step out. On a bus or a plane, even if I’m technically stuck there for a while, my mind doesn’t seem to react the same way. So no, I haven’t really noticed that outside of elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a situation where you were confined, but it wasn't an elevator?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I know I’ve kind of said that already, but I’m trying to answer it honestly and not just give you the quick answer.\n\nIf it’s just a confined place, like a small room or being somewhere crowded, I might not love it, but I don’t get scared in that same way. I can usually tell myself I can leave, or there’s a door, or it’s just temporary. It doesn’t grab me the same way.\n\nWith an elevator, it’s different because once the doors close, I feel like I can’t do anything about it right then. That’s the part that seems to set me off. But other confined situations… I can’t really think of one where I’ve felt truly scared or panicky like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a tunnel or a bridge, where you couldn't easily turn back or exit?\n\nPatient: Tunnels or bridges… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve driven through tunnels and over bridges, and sometimes you’re kind of committed once you’re on them, especially if there’s traffic, but I can’t remember feeling scared from that.\n\nMaybe a little tense if traffic is bad or if people are driving stupid, but that feels more like normal driving stress, not that trapped feeling. I don’t sit there thinking, “I can’t get out, I need to escape,” or anything like that.\n\nWith bridges especially, I’m usually just paying attention to the road. And tunnels, I guess they’re enclosed, but for some reason they don’t hit me the same way. I don’t really notice myself worrying about not being able to turn back or exit. So… no, I wouldn’t say tunnels or bridges are a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or scared in a small, enclosed space with no clear exit, such as a crowded subway car?\n\nPatient: A crowded subway car… I’m not totally sure, because I don’t ride subways much, honestly. If I’m picturing it, I can see how that would be uncomfortable — like if people are pressed in and you can’t move around much — but I don’t know that I’ve had that same scared feeling in that kind of situation.\n\nSmall enclosed spaces in general don’t always bother me just because they’re small. Like, if there’s a door or if I know I can step out, I’m usually okay. Crowds can be annoying, and I don’t like feeling shoved in or like people are too close, but that’s more irritation or wanting some space, not fear.\n\nThe part that really seems different for me is when I feel like I’m sealed in and can’t just get out when I want to. But with something like a subway car, I don’t have a clear memory of panicking or being scared in one. So I guess my answer is… no, not that I’ve noticed, or at least not enough that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you couldn't escape, but it wasn't related to an elevator?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I know it might sound like I’m being repetitive, but I’m trying to sort through it in my head because “couldn’t escape” can mean a few different things, you know?\n\nLike, I’ve been in situations where I couldn’t just walk away immediately — traffic, waiting in line, being in a crowded place, being stuck somewhere for work — and I might get annoyed or impatient. But I don’t remember feeling actually trapped or scared in the way I think you mean.\n\nFor me, if it’s not the elevator kind of situation, I can usually reason it out. Like, there’s an exit somewhere, or eventually the line moves, or the traffic clears, or I can step outside if I really need to. It doesn’t turn into that same feeling of, “I have to get out right now.” So… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening outside of elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt trapped or anxious in a situation where you couldn't escape, but it was not related to an elevator or any mode of transportation?\n\nPatient: Outside of elevators and transportation… I’m trying to think. I don’t think so, no. Like if I’m in a small room or something at work, or in somebody’s office, or even like a closet-type area, I don’t usually feel trapped as long as there’s a door and I know I can leave.\n\nI guess I can get uncomfortable if a place is cramped or people are kind of in my way, but it’s more like, “Okay, move, I need space,” not fear. I don’t get that anxious feeling just from being somewhere enclosed if I know there’s some way out.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really point to a time like that that wasn’t an elevator or a vehicle or something. Nothing comes to mind where I felt stuck and scared in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or trapped in a situation where you couldn't escape, but it was not related to an elevator, transportation, or any physical space?\n\nPatient: Um… not related to a place at all? I’m not sure I’ve had that. I mean, I’ve felt stuck in life situations before, like anybody does — bills, work stuff, dealing with people when you can’t just magically make them stop talking — but that’s more stress or frustration, not like that trapped anxious feeling.\n\nWhen you say “couldn’t escape,” my mind goes straight to actually being physically stuck somewhere, so if you mean something more like a conversation or an obligation or a situation I can’t get out of emotionally, I don’t think it hits me the same way. I might want to be done with it, or I might get irritated, but I don’t feel scared like I can’t breathe or like I need to get out right that second.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve noticed. If it’s not an actual physical stuck kind of thing, I don’t really think of it as feeling trapped.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
